People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Even if someone’s waiting or I’m already late.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel anxious when you're unable to perform your checking rituals?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 19 / taxi_driver / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"Before I can take a ride, I keep going around the car checking the tires, lights, doors, meter, and paperwork over and over, sometimes taking pictures and restarting the whole check if it doesn't feel right, even when passengers are waiting and I'm already late.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. I barely see friends anymore because I'm either behind on work or trying to catch up after the checking delays.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Even if someone’s waiting or I’m already late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're unable to perform your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I get really tense if I can’t do it. Like before a ride, I need to go around the car checking the tires, lights, doors, meter, papers… and if I can’t, or if it doesn’t feel right, I keep thinking I missed something. Sometimes I take pictures or start over, even if people are waiting.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things other than the safety of your passengers or the condition of the cab?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really. It’s mostly the cab stuff, I think. I haven’t really noticed other things like that.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking rituals affecting your relationships or work as a cab driver?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been causing problems.\n\nI’ve lost rides because I can’t get on the road fast enough, and I’ve gotten warnings for it. My CDL studying keeps getting pushed back too, because a lot of the day gets eaten up with checking.\n\nAnd… I don’t really see my friends much anymore. I’m usually behind on work or trying to catch up after I wasted time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts and checking rituals are reasonable, or do they seem excessive or irrational to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it’s too much. Like, checking the tires and lights once makes sense, but I keep going around again and again, doors, meter, papers… sometimes taking pictures or starting the whole thing over if it feels wrong.\n\nSo yeah, I guess it’s excessive. I just don’t feel okay stopping, even when people are waiting and I’m already late.\n\nDoctor: How distressing are these thoughts and checking rituals to you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Pretty distressing… most days.\n\nIt’s like, even after I know I checked, the thought comes back that I missed something on the cab and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied about checking it. I can’t really shut it off, even when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and friendships, that you feel are being affected by these thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the same stuff… work, school stuff, friends.\n\nI mean, the CDL studying is a big one. I keep meaning to do it, but then I’m late from checking and the day’s already messed up. And I don’t really see people much because I’m either behind on rides or trying to catch up. So… yeah, it kind of takes over more than I want to admit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "Determine the presence of excessive anxiety outside of the specific context of the cab and driving", "question": "Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about the cab or driving?"}

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting stuck on this thought… like, even after I’ve checked the cab, I still feel like I missed something. Like someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied about checking it. It keeps coming back while I’m driving, and even when I try to study. I can’t really shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make these thoughts go away or to prevent the bad things you're worried about from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Before I take a ride, I’ll check the car. Tires, lights, doors, the meter, papers… stuff like that.\n\nBut I don’t just do it once. I keep going around again, and sometimes I take pictures. If it doesn’t feel right, I start over. Even if someone’s waiting or I’m already late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're unable to perform your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I get really tense if I can’t do it. Like before a ride, I need to go around the car checking the tires, lights, doors, meter, papers… and if I can’t, or if it doesn’t feel right, I keep thinking I missed something. Sometimes I take pictures or start over, even if people are waiting.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things other than the safety of your passengers or the condition of the cab?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really. It’s mostly the cab stuff, I think. I haven’t really noticed other things like that.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking rituals affecting your relationships or work as a cab driver?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been causing problems.\n\nI’ve lost rides because I can’t get on the road fast enough, and I’ve gotten warnings for it. My CDL studying keeps getting pushed back too, because a lot of the day gets eaten up with checking.\n\nAnd… I don’t really see my friends much anymore. I’m usually behind on work or trying to catch up after I wasted time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts and checking rituals are reasonable, or do they seem excessive or irrational to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it’s too much. Like, checking the tires and lights once makes sense, but I keep going around again and again, doors, meter, papers… sometimes taking pictures or starting the whole thing over if it feels wrong.\n\nSo yeah, I guess it’s excessive. I just don’t feel okay stopping, even when people are waiting and I’m already late.\n\nDoctor: How distressing are these thoughts and checking rituals to you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Pretty distressing… most days.\n\nIt’s like, even after I know I checked, the thought comes back that I missed something on the cab and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied about checking it. I can’t really shut it off, even when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and friendships, that you feel are being affected by these thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the same stuff… work, school stuff, friends.\n\nI mean, the CDL studying is a big one. I keep meaning to do it, but then I’m late from checking and the day’s already messed up. And I don’t really see people much because I’m either behind on rides or trying to catch up. So… yeah, it kind of takes over more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about the cab or driving?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I guess.\n\nMost of it is tied to the cab stuff. If I’m not thinking about that, I’m usually just… okay, or distracted. I haven’t really noticed being on edge for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the cab, like when you're relaxing or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, if I’m actually doing something else, I don’t think I’m anxious. I haven’t noticed it much outside of the work stuff.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m usually behind on work or trying to catch up after I wasted time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts and checking rituals are reasonable, or do they seem excessive or irrational to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it’s too much. Like, checking the tires and lights once makes sense, but I keep going around again and again, doors, meter, papers… sometimes taking pictures or starting the whole thing over if it feels wrong.\n\nSo yeah, I guess it’s excessive. I just don’t feel okay stopping, even when people are waiting and I’m already late.\n\nDoctor: How distressing are these thoughts and checking rituals to you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Pretty distressing… most days.\n\nIt’s like, even after I know I checked, the thought comes back that I missed something on the cab and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied about checking it. I can’t really shut it off, even when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and friendships, that you feel are being affected by these thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the same stuff… work, school stuff, friends.\n\nI mean, the CDL studying is a big one. I keep meaning to do it, but then I’m late from checking and the day’s already messed up. And I don’t really see people much because I’m either behind on rides or trying to catch up. So… yeah, it kind of takes over more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about the cab or driving?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I guess.\n\nMost of it is tied to the cab stuff. If I’m not thinking about that, I’m usually just… okay, or distracted. I haven’t really noticed being on edge for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the cab, like when you're relaxing or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, if I’m actually doing something else, I don’t think I’m anxious. I haven’t noticed it much outside of the work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with family?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t really noticed feeling anxious during that kind of stuff. If I’m distracted, I’m usually just… distracted.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"intent": "Determine if the patient's anxiety is exclusively related to the obsessive thoughts", "question": "Is your anxiety only present when you're thinking about the cab or is it also there at other times?"}

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting stuck on this thought… like, even after I’ve checked the cab, I still feel like I missed something. Like someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied about checking it. It keeps coming back while I’m driving, and even when I try to study. I can’t really shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make these thoughts go away or to prevent the bad things you're worried about from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Before I take a ride, I’ll check the car. Tires, lights, doors, the meter, papers… stuff like that.\n\nBut I don’t just do it once. I keep going around again, and sometimes I take pictures. If it doesn’t feel right, I start over. Even if someone’s waiting or I’m already late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're unable to perform your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I get really tense if I can’t do it. Like before a ride, I need to go around the car checking the tires, lights, doors, meter, papers… and if I can’t, or if it doesn’t feel right, I keep thinking I missed something. Sometimes I take pictures or start over, even if people are waiting.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things other than the safety of your passengers or the condition of the cab?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really. It’s mostly the cab stuff, I think. I haven’t really noticed other things like that.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking rituals affecting your relationships or work as a cab driver?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been causing problems.\n\nI’ve lost rides because I can’t get on the road fast enough, and I’ve gotten warnings for it. My CDL studying keeps getting pushed back too, because a lot of the day gets eaten up with checking.\n\nAnd… I don’t really see my friends much anymore. I’m usually behind on work or trying to catch up after I wasted time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts and checking rituals are reasonable, or do they seem excessive or irrational to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it’s too much. Like, checking the tires and lights once makes sense, but I keep going around again and again, doors, meter, papers… sometimes taking pictures or starting the whole thing over if it feels wrong.\n\nSo yeah, I guess it’s excessive. I just don’t feel okay stopping, even when people are waiting and I’m already late.\n\nDoctor: How distressing are these thoughts and checking rituals to you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Pretty distressing… most days.\n\nIt’s like, even after I know I checked, the thought comes back that I missed something on the cab and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied about checking it. I can’t really shut it off, even when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and friendships, that you feel are being affected by these thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the same stuff… work, school stuff, friends.\n\nI mean, the CDL studying is a big one. I keep meaning to do it, but then I’m late from checking and the day’s already messed up. And I don’t really see people much because I’m either behind on rides or trying to catch up. So… yeah, it kind of takes over more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about the cab or driving?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I guess.\n\nMost of it is tied to the cab stuff. If I’m not thinking about that, I’m usually just… okay, or distracted. I haven’t really noticed being on edge for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the cab, like when you're relaxing or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, if I’m actually doing something else, I don’t think I’m anxious. I haven’t noticed it much outside of the work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with family?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t really noticed feeling anxious during that kind of stuff. If I’m distracted, I’m usually just… distracted.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only present when you're thinking about the cab or is it also there at other times?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly when I start thinking about the cab. Like… even if I know I checked it, I get this thought that I missed something and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied. Then it’s hard to shut off, especially when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the cab and there's no apparent reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve really noticed.   },

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting stuck on this thought… like, even after I’ve checked the cab, I still feel like I missed something. Like someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied about checking it. It keeps coming back while I’m driving, and even when I try to study. I can’t really shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make these thoughts go away or to prevent the bad things you're worried about from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Before I take a ride, I’ll check the car. Tires, lights, doors, the meter, papers… stuff like that.\n\nBut I don’t just do it once. I keep going around again, and sometimes I take pictures. If it doesn’t feel right, I start over. Even if someone’s waiting or I’m already late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're unable to perform your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I get really tense if I can’t do it. Like before a ride, I need to go around the car checking the tires, lights, doors, meter, papers… and if I can’t, or if it doesn’t feel right, I keep thinking I missed something. Sometimes I take pictures or start over, even if people are waiting.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things other than the safety of your passengers or the condition of the cab?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really. It’s mostly the cab stuff, I think. I haven’t really noticed other things like that.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking rituals affecting your relationships or work as a cab driver?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been causing problems.\n\nI’ve lost rides because I can’t get on the road fast enough, and I’ve gotten warnings for it. My CDL studying keeps getting pushed back too, because a lot of the day gets eaten up with checking.\n\nAnd… I don’t really see my friends much anymore. I’m usually behind on work or trying to catch up after I wasted time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts and checking rituals are reasonable, or do they seem excessive or irrational to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it’s too much. Like, checking the tires and lights once makes sense, but I keep going around again and again, doors, meter, papers… sometimes taking pictures or starting the whole thing over if it feels wrong.\n\nSo yeah, I guess it’s excessive. I just don’t feel okay stopping, even when people are waiting and I’m already late.\n\nDoctor: How distressing are these thoughts and checking rituals to you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Pretty distressing… most days.\n\nIt’s like, even after I know I checked, the thought comes back that I missed something on the cab and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied about checking it. I can’t really shut it off, even when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and friendships, that you feel are being affected by these thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the same stuff… work, school stuff, friends.\n\nI mean, the CDL studying is a big one. I keep meaning to do it, but then I’m late from checking and the day’s already messed up. And I don’t really see people much because I’m either behind on rides or trying to catch up. So… yeah, it kind of takes over more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about the cab or driving?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I guess.\n\nMost of it is tied to the cab stuff. If I’m not thinking about that, I’m usually just… okay, or distracted. I haven’t really noticed being on edge for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the cab, like when you're relaxing or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, if I’m actually doing something else, I don’t think I’m anxious. I haven’t noticed it much outside of the work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with family?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t really noticed feeling anxious during that kind of stuff. If I’m distracted, I’m usually just… distracted.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only present when you're thinking about the cab or is it also there at other times?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly when I start thinking about the cab. Like… even if I know I checked it, I get this thought that I missed something and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied. Then it’s hard to shut off, especially when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the cab and there's no apparent reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve really noticed. It usually seems tied to those thoughts, not just out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious in situations that have nothing to do with your work as a cab driver?\n\nPatient: Not really… it’s mainly that cab thing. Like even after I’ve checked it, I keep thinking I missed something and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied. It gets stuck in my head when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in social situations, like when you're meeting new people?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can be quiet around new people, but I don’t think I get anxious like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is only about the cab and your work, or is it something that affects you more broadly in life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the cab and work. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting stuck on this thought… like, even after I’ve checked the cab, I still feel like I missed something. Like someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied about checking it. It keeps coming back while I’m driving, and even when I try to study. I can’t really shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make these thoughts go away or to prevent the bad things you're worried about from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Before I take a ride, I’ll check the car. Tires, lights, doors, the meter, papers… stuff like that.\n\nBut I don’t just do it once. I keep going around again, and sometimes I take pictures. If it doesn’t feel right, I start over. Even if someone’s waiting or I’m already late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're unable to perform your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I get really tense if I can’t do it. Like before a ride, I need to go around the car checking the tires, lights, doors, meter, papers… and if I can’t, or if it doesn’t feel right, I keep thinking I missed something. Sometimes I take pictures or start over, even if people are waiting.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things other than the safety of your passengers or the condition of the cab?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really. It’s mostly the cab stuff, I think. I haven’t really noticed other things like that.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking rituals affecting your relationships or work as a cab driver?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been causing problems.\n\nI’ve lost rides because I can’t get on the road fast enough, and I’ve gotten warnings for it. My CDL studying keeps getting pushed back too, because a lot of the day gets eaten up with checking.\n\nAnd… I don’t really see my friends much anymore. I’m usually behind on work or trying to catch up after I wasted time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts and checking rituals are reasonable, or do they seem excessive or irrational to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it’s too much. Like, checking the tires and lights once makes sense, but I keep going around again and again, doors, meter, papers… sometimes taking pictures or starting the whole thing over if it feels wrong.\n\nSo yeah, I guess it’s excessive. I just don’t feel okay stopping, even when people are waiting and I’m already late.\n\nDoctor: How distressing are these thoughts and checking rituals to you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Pretty distressing… most days.\n\nIt’s like, even after I know I checked, the thought comes back that I missed something on the cab and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied about checking it. I can’t really shut it off, even when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and friendships, that you feel are being affected by these thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the same stuff… work, school stuff, friends.\n\nI mean, the CDL studying is a big one. I keep meaning to do it, but then I’m late from checking and the day’s already messed up. And I don’t really see people much because I’m either behind on rides or trying to catch up. So… yeah, it kind of takes over more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about the cab or driving?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I guess.\n\nMost of it is tied to the cab stuff. If I’m not thinking about that, I’m usually just… okay, or distracted. I haven’t really noticed being on edge for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the cab, like when you're relaxing or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, if I’m actually doing something else, I don’t think I’m anxious. I haven’t noticed it much outside of the work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with family?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t really noticed feeling anxious during that kind of stuff. If I’m distracted, I’m usually just… distracted.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only present when you're thinking about the cab or is it also there at other times?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly when I start thinking about the cab. Like… even if I know I checked it, I get this thought that I missed something and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied. Then it’s hard to shut off, especially when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the cab and there's no apparent reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve really noticed. It usually seems tied to those thoughts, not just out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious in situations that have nothing to do with your work as a cab driver?\n\nPatient: Not really… it’s mainly that cab thing. Like even after I’ve checked it, I keep thinking I missed something and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied. It gets stuck in my head when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in social situations, like when you're meeting new people?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can be quiet around new people, but I don’t think I get anxious like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is only about the cab and your work, or is it something that affects you more broadly in life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the cab and work. Like… I came in because even after I know I did the safety check, I keep getting hit with the thought that I missed something, and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied. It just won’t really shut off when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are in a completely different environment, away from work and the cab?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know… I haven’t noticed it much when I’m away from the cab. It’s not something I usually think about, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I can’t really shut it off, even when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and friendships, that you feel are being affected by these thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the same stuff… work, school stuff, friends.\n\nI mean, the CDL studying is a big one. I keep meaning to do it, but then I’m late from checking and the day’s already messed up. And I don’t really see people much because I’m either behind on rides or trying to catch up. So… yeah, it kind of takes over more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about the cab or driving?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I guess.\n\nMost of it is tied to the cab stuff. If I’m not thinking about that, I’m usually just… okay, or distracted. I haven’t really noticed being on edge for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the cab, like when you're relaxing or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, if I’m actually doing something else, I don’t think I’m anxious. I haven’t noticed it much outside of the work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with family?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t really noticed feeling anxious during that kind of stuff. If I’m distracted, I’m usually just… distracted.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only present when you're thinking about the cab or is it also there at other times?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly when I start thinking about the cab. Like… even if I know I checked it, I get this thought that I missed something and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied. Then it’s hard to shut off, especially when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the cab and there's no apparent reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve really noticed. It usually seems tied to those thoughts, not just out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious in situations that have nothing to do with your work as a cab driver?\n\nPatient: Not really… it’s mainly that cab thing. Like even after I’ve checked it, I keep thinking I missed something and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied. It gets stuck in my head when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in social situations, like when you're meeting new people?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can be quiet around new people, but I don’t think I get anxious like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is only about the cab and your work, or is it something that affects you more broadly in life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the cab and work. Like… I came in because even after I know I did the safety check, I keep getting hit with the thought that I missed something, and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied. It just won’t really shut off when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are in a completely different environment, away from work and the cab?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know… I haven’t noticed it much when I’m away from the cab. It’s not something I usually think about, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the cab and there's no apparent reason for it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so. If I’m not thinking about that stuff, I don’t really notice anxiety coming up for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel anxious and it has nothing to do with the cab or your work as a driver?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can’t think of a time like that. It usually ties back to the cab or work somehow.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
